30:["$","p",null,{"children":"The LUCJ ansatz adapts to QPUs with restricted qubit connectivity. The spin orbitals are mapped to qubits such that the ansatz does not require routing with SWAP gates. IBM® hardware has either square lattice or heavy-hex lattice qubit topology. For the heavy-hex lattice, we can adopt a \"zig-zag\" pattern depicted below. In this pattern, orbitals with the same spin are mapped to qubits with a line topology (red and blue circles), and a connection between orbitals of different spin is present at every 4th spatial orbital, with the connection being facilitated by an ancilla qubit (purple circles).","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]

33:["$","p",null,{"children":["The self-consistent configuration recovery procedure is designed to extract as much signal as possible from noisy quantum samples. Because the molecular Hamiltonian conserves particle number and spin Z, it makes sense to choose a circuit ansatz that also conserves these symmetries. When applied to the Hartree-Fock state, the resulting state has a fixed particle number and spin Z in the noiseless setting. Therefore, the spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"α"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\alpha"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.4306em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0037em"},"children":"α"}]]}]}]]}]," and spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"β"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\beta"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.8889em","verticalAlign":"-0.1944em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.05278em"},"children":"β"}]]}]}]]}]," halves of any bitstring sampled from this state should have the same ",["$","$L1d",null,{"href":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamming_weight","children":"Hamming weight"}]," as in the Hartree-Fock state. Due to the presence of noise in current quantum processors, some measured bitstrings will violate this property. A simple form of postselection would discard these bitstrings, but this is wasteful because those bitstrings might still contain some signal. The self-consistent recovery procedure attempts to recover some of that signal in post-processing. The procedure is iterative and requires as input an estimate of the average occupancies of each orbital in the ground state, which is first computed from the raw samples. The procedure is run in a loop, and each iteration has the following steps:"],"className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
34:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"For each bitstring that violates the specified symmetries, flip its bits with a probabilistic procedure designed to bring the bitstring closer to the current estimate of the average orbital occupancies, to obtain a new bitstring.","className":"my-12"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Collect all of the old and new bitstrings that satisfy the symmetries, and divide a subsample of them into disjoint batches of a fixed size, chosen in advance.","className":"my-12"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["For each batch of bitstrings, project the Hamiltonian into the subspace spanned by the corresponding basis vectors (see the ",["$","$L1d",null,{"href":"#quantum-chemistry","children":"previous section"}]," for a description of these basis vectors), and compute a ground state estimate of the projected Hamiltonian on a classical computer. Each bitstring consists of a spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"α"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\alpha"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.4306em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0037em"},"children":"α"}]]}]}]]}]," half and a spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"β"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\beta"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.8889em","verticalAlign":"-0.1944em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.05278em"},"children":"β"}]]}]}]]}]," half, each of which we call a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"CI string"}],". The subspace for a batch is spanned by the basis vectors formed by pairing every spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"α"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\alpha"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.4306em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0037em"},"children":"α"}]]}]}]]}]," CI string in the batch with every spin-",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"β"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\beta"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.8889em","verticalAlign":"-0.1944em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.05278em"},"children":"β"}]]}]}]]}]," CI string in the batch."],"className":"my-12"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","em",null,{"children":"Trim"}]," each batch, keeping only the CI strings that carry the largest weight in its ground state estimate. Merge the CI strings that survive from all of the batches, and diagonalize the Hamiltonian once more in the merged subspace."],"className":"my-12"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Update the estimate of the average orbital occupancies using the ground state estimate from the merged subspace. Its highest-weight CI strings are also carried over into the subspaces of the next iteration.","className":"my-12"}],"\n"],"className":"list-outside ml-32 list-decimal tabular-nums"}]
35:["$","p",null,{"children":["Trimming and merging the batches in step 4 is the ",["$","$L1d",null,{"href":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01138","children":"trim SQD"}]," variant of the algorithm. Because diagonalizing a batch to screen its CI strings is cheaper than diagonalizing a subspace large enough to hold every candidate CI string, trimming lets SQD select from a larger pool of sampled CI strings than it could diagonalize at once."],"className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
